Exponentiable virtual double categories and presheaves for double categories Thanks: The author was supported by a departmental postdoctoral grant from the Department of Software Science at Tallinn University of Technology.
Nathanael Arkor

Abstract
. Given a pair of pseudo double categories 𝔸 {\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}} and 𝔹 {\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}} , the lax functors from 𝔸 {\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}} to 𝔹 {\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}} , along with their transformations, modules, and multimodulations, assemble into a virtual double category 𝕃 ​ 𝐚𝐱 ​ ( 𝔸 , 𝔹 ) {\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}) . We exhibit a universal property of this construction by observing that it arises naturally from the consideration of exponentiability for virtual double categories. In particular, we show that every pseudo double category is exponentiable as a virtual double category, whereby the virtual double category 𝕃 ​ 𝐚𝐱 ​ ( 𝔸 , 𝔹 ) {\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}) of lax functors arises as the virtual double category 𝕄 ​ 𝐨𝐝 ​ ( 𝔹 𝔸 ) {\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}) of monads and modules in the exponential 𝔹 𝔸 {\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}} . We explore some consequences of this characterisation, demonstrating that it facilitates simple proofs of statements that heretofore required unwieldy computations. For instance, we deduce that the 2-category of pseudo double categories and lax functors is enriched in the 2-category of normal virtual double categories, and demonstrate that several aspects of the Yoneda theory of pseudo double categories – such as the correspondence between presheaves and discrete fibrations – are substantially simplified by this perspective.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
18D15,18D20,18D60,18M65,18N10

1.
Introduction

In the last twenty years, it has become increasingly clear that weak double categories play a fundamental role in category theory
[
GP99
,
GP04
,
Shu08
]
. However, due to its relative youth, several basic aspects of the theory of double categories have remained underdeveloped. One such aspect is the theory of
representability
for double categories. The notion of
presheaf
for double categories was first studied in detail by
[
Par11
]
, who showed that, for each double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, there exists a fully faithful functor
Y_{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}})
, sending each object in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
to a contravariant lax functor from
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
into the double category of sets and spans; the appropriateness of this definition is justified by a corresponding Yoneda lemma. The generalisation from categories to double categories is by no means routine: for a category
\mathbf{A}
, the presheaves on
\mathbf{A}
assemble into a category
{\mathbf{Set}}^{\mathbf{A}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
; on the other hand,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}})
is, in general, not a pseudo double category but merely a
virtual double category
– a structure which generalises a double category in the same way that a multicategory generalises a monoidal category. Thus, surprisingly, the theory of double categories appears to lead to the study of virtual double categories.

While the development of
[
Par11
]
is powerful and insightful, the nature of the virtual double category of presheaves is left rather mysterious. For a category
\mathbf{A}
, the Yoneda embedding
y_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathbf{A}\to{\mathbf{Set}}^{\mathbf{A}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
corresponds, via the cartesian closed structure of the 2-category
\mathbf{Cat}
, to the hom-functor
\mathbf{A}({-},{-})\colon\mathbf{A}{}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{A}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
. This gives a particularly elementary perspective on the Yoneda embedding, every category being equipped with a hom-functor essentially by definition. We might therefore hope that something similar is true for the double categorical Yoneda embedding
Y_{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}})
: namely, that it corresponds, via some notion of ‘transposition’, to the lax hom-functor
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}({-},{-})\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}
. The present paper stemmed from the desire to understand to what extent this is true.

Exponentiability of virtual double categories

Given that, for
\mathbf{Cat}
, transposition is induced by cartesian closure, a first instinct might be to guess that the 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
of virtual double categories is cartesian closed and that, for each pair
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}
of double categories, the virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
of lax functors forms the exponential
{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
. This is false on both counts, but nevertheless turns out to be a fruitful thought. To understand what is going on, we take inspiration from the theory of multicategories. Just as monoidal categories may be seen as double categories with a single object and single tight morphism, so too may multicategories be seen as virtual double categories with a single object and single tight morphism, and in many respects the theory of virtual double categories may thereby be seen to extend the theory of multicategories. The 2-category of multicategories is
not
cartesian closed, but it does admit some exponentials:
[
Pis14
]
established that a multicategory is exponentiable if and only if it is
promonoidal
in the sense of
[
Day70
]
. In particular, every monoidal category is promonoidal, hence exponentiable as a multicategory. Given monoidal categories
{\mathbf{M}}
and
\mathbf{N}
, one might thus expect that their exponential
\mathbf{N}^{\mathbf{M}}
in the category of multicategories to comprise the lax monoidal functors from
{\mathbf{M}}
to
\mathbf{N}
. This is not true; however, we can obtain the lax monoidal functors from
{\mathbf{M}}
to
\mathbf{N}
as the
monoids
in
\mathbf{N}^{\mathbf{M}}
.

Guided by the one-object setting, we carry out a similar analysis for virtual double categories. As for multicategories, while the 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is not cartesian closed, every double category is exponentiable and so, given double categories
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}
, we may form an exponential virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
. The analogue of a monoid in a multicategory is a monad in a virtual double category
1
1

1

In this paper, a
monad
in a virtual double category will always refer to a
loose monad
, rather than a
tight monad
(which can only be defined in a virtual double category with enough identity loose morphisms).
, and for every virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, there is a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
of monads and modules in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.
Our main theorem establishes the expected analogue
2
2

2

In fact, the virtual double categorical story is better behaved than the multicategorical one, as the monoids in a multicategory merely form a category, rather than a multicategory, whereas the monads in a virtual double category form another virtual double category.
of the multicategorical story.

Theorem

(
Theorem

6.2
)
.

[
Par11
]
’s virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})
.

In answer to our original question,
[
Par11
]
’s Yoneda embedding may be decomposed into the following composite,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}^{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}})}}

in which the first functor sends each object in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
to the identity monad on that object, and in which the second functor is the image under
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
of the transposition
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}^{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}}
of the lax hom-functor
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}({-},{-})\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}
. Thus, the Yoneda embedding is obtained, as desired, by transposition of the hom-functor, ‘up to
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
’.

Enrichment and universality

While our given motivation for such a characterisation of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
was entirely conceptual, it turns out to have a number of practical implications. For instance, it follows easily (
Corollary

6.6
) that the 2-category of double categories and lax functors is enriched in the 2-category of
normal
virtual double categories (which are virtual double categories that admit identity loose morphisms). Furthermore, the virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
acquires a universal property from the universal properties of the
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
construction
[
CS10
]
and the exponential
{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, equipping the 2-category of virtual double categories with a partially-closed skew-monoidal structure (
Theorem

6.10
). As a consequence, it becomes essentially unnecessary to manipulate
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
directly; the benefits of this will be immediately apparent to readers familiar with previous work on virtual double categories of lax functors
[
Par11
,
Par13
,
Lam21
]
.

Representability for double categories

Having answered our motivating question, we explore the extent to which this perspective simplifies and clarifies the theory of representability for double categories developed by
[
Par11
]
. One of the most important aspects of category theory is the relationship between families and bundles. For categories, this relationship takes the form of an equivalence between the category of presheaves on a fixed category
\mathbf{A}
and the category of discrete fibrations over
\mathbf{A}
. The situation for double categories is similar.
[
Par11
]
observed that every lax functor
F\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}
admits the construction of a double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{E}l}}(F)
of elements, equipped with a projection functor
\pi_{F}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{E}l}}(F)\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, which is a discrete fibration of double categories. Subsequently,
[
Lam21
]
, established that, for
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
a
strict
double category, this assignment extends to an equivalence between the virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}})
and a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}Fib}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})
of discrete fibrations over
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
. Unexpectedly, the assumption that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
be strict is not simply a matter of convenience, but rather is crucial in
[
Lam21
]
’s proof strategy; we shall elaborate on this matter in
Section

8
. Later,
[
CLPS22
]
extended the correspondence to permit
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
to be an arbitrary pseudo double category, but merely established an equivalence of categories rather than of virtual double categories.

To demonstrate the utility of our characterisation of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
, we give in
Section

8
a new proof of the correspondence between presheaves on double categories and discrete fibrations of double categories. Our strategy has several advantages over previous approaches. For instance, we establish an equivalence of virtual double categories, rather than merely of categories, with respect to an arbitrary pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
. Our correspondence thus strictly generalises the previous correspondences. Furthermore, our proof strategy is entirely two-dimensional in nature and avoids manual calculation, by deriving the correspondence directly from that for categories, in contrast to
[
Lam21
]
and
[
CLPS22
]
respectively.

1.1.
Outline of paper

We begin in
Section

2
by recalling the basic definitions of double category theory (both pseudo and virtual) that we will use throughout the paper.
In
Section

3
, we study exponentiability for virtual double categories and, in particular, establish that pseudo double categories are exponentiable virtual double categories (
Theorem

3.9
). In
Section

4
, we recall the definition of the virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
of monads and modules in a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, recall its universal property as the cofree normal virtual double category on
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
(
Proposition

4.5
), and study some of its properties, including its lax-idempotence (
Section

4.2
) and its interaction with the slice construction (
Section

4.3
). In
Section

5
, we recall the construction of the free normal virtual double category on
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}

[
FL25
]
, and establish some properties useful for the following sections. In
Section

6
, we establish our main result, demonstrating that
[
Par11
]
’s virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
of lax functors between pseudo double categories arises as
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})
(
Theorem

6.2
). The remainder of the paper is dedicated to exploring consequences of this result. For instance, we show that the 2-category of pseudo double categories and lax functors is enriched in the 2-category of (normal) virtual double categories (
Corollary

6.6
), and exhibit a universal property of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
(
Theorem

6.10
). In
Section

7
, we study representability for presheaves of double categories, and show that our perspective simplifies aspects of the development of
[
Par11
]
. Finally, in
Section

8
, we use the perspective to give a new and substantially simpler proof that contravariant
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}
-valued lax functors from a pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
are equivalent to discrete fibrations over
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, generalising the main theorem of
[
Lam21
]
from strict double categories to pseudo double categories.

1.2.
Tight representability and loose representability

Above, and in this paper as a whole, we focus on just one aspect of representability for double categories. However, we find it helpful to describe briefly how this paper fits into the larger picture, by clarifying the relationship between two approaches for representability for double categories that may be found in the literature.

A presheaf on a category is most helpfully considered as a special case of a more general concept: a
distributor
between categories, which provides a notion of
heteromorphism
between objects of different categories
[
Bén73
]
. Since there is a single notion of morphism between objects of a category, there is a single notion of distributor between categories. However, double categories have two kinds of morphism – the
tight morphisms
(which compose strictly), and the
loose morphisms
(which compose weakly) – and correspondingly there are two notions of distributor between double categories:
tight distributors
and
loose distributors
, respectively providing notions of
tight heteromorphisms
and
loose heteromorphisms
between objects of different double categories
3
3

3

Tight and loose distributors may be defined efficiently as (necessarily strict) functors of double categories into the free-standing tight morphism and into the free-standing loose morphism respectively. The reader is invited to verify that these two definitions recover the notions of (tight) presheaf of
[
Par11
]
and (loose) presheaf of
[
FGK12
]
respectively.
. By specialising to (tight or loose) distributors with terminal domain or codomain, we obtain notions of
tight (co)presheaf
and
loose (co)presheaf
.

This reveals there are two notions of representability for double categories:
tight representability
, which is concerned with the representability of tight presheaves (and, more generally, tight distributors); and
loose representability
, which is concerned with the representability of loose presheaves (and, more generally, loose distributors).
[
Par11
]
’s
[
Par11
]
presheaves for double categories – namely, lax functors
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}
– are the
tight
presheaves. Consequently, we focus in this paper purely on tight representability.

Loose representability has been considered in the literature by
[
FGK12
, §3]
, albeit only in the context of strict double categories. The precise connection between the two approaches deserves further clarification.

1.3.
Acknowledgements

The author thanks Bryce Clarke for helpful discussions during the writing of the paper, and Bryce Clarke and Dylan McDermott for comments on the paper itself.

2.
Pseudo double categories and virtual double categories

We begin by recalling the basic definitions of pseudo and virtual double categories, both for the benefit of the reader unfamiliar with these notions, and to establish our terminology and notation. The reader familiar with these concepts may prefer to skip directly to
Section

3
.

The central concept in this paper is that of a
virtual double category
, which is to a multicategory what a pseudo double category is to a monoidal category. Like a double category , a virtual double category is a structure with two kinds of morphism, which we call
tight morphisms
and
loose morphisms
. The objects and tight morphisms in a virtual double category assemble into a category, so that tight morphisms compose strictly associatively and unitally. However, it is not generally possible to compose the loose morphisms in a virtual double category. Instead, a virtual double category has
cells
that are framed at the top by a path of loose morphisms, representing a ‘virtual composite’. If you are encountering the definition for the first time, this may appear a little intimidating. However, in a virtual double category, unlike in a pseudo double category, there is no coherence data, and it often turns out to be simpler to work with virtual double categories than with pseudo double categories for this reason.

Definition 2.1

(
[
Bur71
,
CS10
]
)
.

A
virtual double category

{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
comprises the following data.

(2.1.(1))

A category
\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}
of
objects
and
tight morphisms
.
We denote a tight morphism
f
from an object
A
to an object
B
by an arrow
f\colon A\to B
; denote the composition of tight morphisms
f\colon A\to B
and
g\colon B\to C
both by
(f\mathbin{;}g)\colon A\to C
and by
gf\colon A\to C
; and denote the identity of an object
A
by
1_{A}\colon A\to A
, or simply by
=
in pasting diagrams.

(2.1.(2))

For each pair of objects
A

loose morphisms
from
A

p
from
A

.

Example 2.2
.

Every category
\mathbf{A}
forms a virtual double category
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
with no loose morphisms or cells.

Example 2.3
.

Every multicategory
{\mathbf{M}}
forms a virtual double category
\Sigma{\mathbf{M}}
with a single object and single tight morphism, the loose morphisms of
\Sigma{\mathbf{M}}
being the objects of
{\mathbf{M}}
, and the cells of
\Sigma{\mathbf{M}}
being the multimorphisms of
{\mathbf{M}}
.

Example 2.4
.

The terminal virtual double category is denoted
\bbn 1
, and has a single object, single tight morphism, single loose morphism, and a single
n
-ary cell for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Example 2.5
.

The virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}
is defined as follows.

(2.5.(1))

The underlying category is
{\mathbf{Set}}
, the category of sets and functions.

(2.5.(2))

A loose morphism from
A
to
B
is a span
A\leftarrow X\to B
.

(2.5.(3))

A cell with the following frame

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

is a span morphism from the iterated pullback, as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}\times_{A_{1}}X_{2}\times_{A_{2}}\cdots\times_{A_{n}}X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

The identity on a span
A\leftarrow X\to B
is given by
1_{X}
, and the composition of cells is induced by the universal property of the pullback in the evident manner.

Example 2.6
.

More generally, for any category
{\mathbf{E}}
with pullbacks, there is a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}({\mathbf{E}})
, whose underlying category is
{\mathbf{E}}
, in which a loose morphism is a span in
{\mathbf{E}}
, and whose cells and their composition is defined analogously to
Example

2.5
. In particular,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}\mathrel{:=}{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}({\mathbf{Set}})
.

Example 2.7
.

The virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}
is defined as follows.

(2.7.(1))

The underlying category is
\mathbf{Cat}
, the category of
small
categories and functors.

(2.7.(2))

A loose morphism from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
is a distributor from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
, i.e. a functor
\mathbf{B}{}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{A}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
.

(2.7.(3))

A cell with the following frame

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{A}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{B}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

comprises a family of functions

\phi_{a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}}\colon p_{1}(a_{0},a_{1})\times\cdots\times p_{n}(a_{n-1},a_{n})\to q(fa_{0},f^{\prime}a_{n})

for each
a_{0}\in|\mathbf{A}_{0}|,\dots,a_{n}\in|\mathbf{A}_{n}|
, satisfying naturality conditions
[
AM24
, Definition 8.1]
.

The identity on a distributor is given by the identity function, and the composition of cells is induced by functoriality of the cartesian product.

Example 2.8
.

More generally, for any category
{\mathbf{E}}
with pullbacks, there is a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}({\mathbf{E}})
, whose underlying category is
\mathbf{Cat}({\mathbf{E}})
(the category of categories and functors internal to
{\mathbf{E}}
), in which a loose morphism is an internal distributor, and whose cells are internal natural transformations. Since we will only refer to this example in passing, we will not spell it out, but will rather define
{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}({\mathbf{E}})
by means of a general construction on
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}({\mathbf{E}})
in
Example

4.2
. In particular,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}\mathrel{:=}{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}({\mathbf{Set}})
.

Definition 2.9

(
[
Bur71
]
)
.

A
functor
of virtual double categories is a homomorphism, having an assignment on objects, tight morphisms, loose morphisms, and cells that (strictly) preserves identities and composites of tight morphisms and cells. Every functor of virtual double categories
F\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}
has an underlying functor
\underline{F}\colon\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}\to\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}}
of categories.

At first,
Definition

2.9
may appear too strict, as we are asking for all structure to be preserved strictly. However, as we shall see in
Theorem

2.15
, such functors correspond to
lax functors
between (non-virtual) double categories. This illustrates the general principle we mentioned in the beginning of the section: that virtual double categories provide a coherence-free approach to double category theory.

Definition 2.10

(
[
DPP06
, 489]
)
.

Let
F,G\colon\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}\to\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}
be functors of virtual double categories. A
transformation

\Phi
from
F
to
G
comprises the following data.

(2.10.(1))

A tight morphism
\Phi_{X}\colon FX\to GX
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
for each object of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

(2.10.(2))

A cell
\Phi_{p}
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
for each loose morphism
p\colon X^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}X
of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{FX^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{GX^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{p}}

The family of tight morphisms is required to form a natural transformation
\underline{F}\Rightarrow\underline{G}
, and the family of cells is required to be natural in the sense that, for each cell
\chi
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as follows,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

the following equation holds in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
.

Proposition 2.11

(
[
DPP06
]
5
5

5

Note that
[
DPP06
]
call virtual double categories
lax double categories
, though, as discussed in
[
Par11
, §4]
, this name is better reserved for a notion of double category in which loose morphisms may be composed, but only associatively and unitally up to noninvertible cells (called a
slack double category
in
[
DPP06
]
).
)
.

Virtual double categories, functors, and transformations form a 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
.

While virtual double categories do not admit composites of loose morphisms in general, we may characterise the existence of particular composites by a universal property, in the same way that we may characterise the existence of certain tensor products in a multicategory.

(

,
CS10
]
)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{opcart}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\phi}}

In this case, we call
q
the
(loose) composite

q_{1}\odot\cdots\odot q_{m}
of
q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
.
When
m=0
, we call
q\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}A
the
loose identity
and denote it by
A(1,1)
, or simply by
=\!\!\!|\!\!\!=
in pasting diagrams.

We denote a nullary loose morphism with loose identity codomain by
\phi\colon f\Rightarrow g
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

A virtual double category is
representable
when it admits all loose composites (including loose identities).

Loose composition is associative and unital up to coherent isomorphism, e.g. we have
(p\odot q)\odot r\cong p\odot q\odot r\cong p\odot(q\odot r)
when each of these composites exist.

Definition 2.13

(
[
DPP06
,
CS10
]
)
.

If the universal property of
Definition

2.12
holds only for
l=0
and
r=0
, we say that the cell is
weakly opcartesian
, and that
q
is a
weak loose composite
or
weak loose identity
. A virtual double category is
weakly representable
when it admits all weak loose composites.

Remark 2.14

(Terminological warning)
.

The term
representability
is used in two senses in this paper. When speaking of pseudo double categories, it is in the sense of
representable presheaves
; whereas, when speaking of virtual double categories, it is in the sense of
Definition

2.12
, i.e. the existence of loose composites.

Pseudo double categories and their morphisms are typically defined explicitly in terms of pseudocategories in the 2-category
\mathbf{Cat}

[
GP99
,
ABK24
]
. For our purposes, it will be more useful to view pseudo double categories as certain kinds of virtual double categories, which is justified by the following result of
[
DPP06
]
.

Theorem 2.15
.

Denote by
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}
the 2-category of pseudo double categories, lax functors, and transformations
[
GP99
, §7.1 – 7.3]
. There is a fully faithful 2-functor
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
whose image comprises the representable virtual double categories.

Proof.

The 2-category
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}
is the Kleisli 2-category for a lax-idempotent 2-comonad on
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{s}

[
DPP06
, Proposition 1.19]
, whereas the 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is the 2-category of strict coalgebras
[
DPP06
, Theorem 2.13]
. The image of the fully faithful inclusion of the Kleisli 2-category into the 2-category of coalgebras comprises the representable virtual double categories
[
DPP06
, Proposition 2.8]
.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

Similarly,
colax double categories
may be identified with the virtual double categories that are weakly representable
[
DPP06
,
CS10
]
.

In this paper, we
define
a
pseudo double category
to be a representable virtual double category and a
lax functor
to be a functor of virtual double categories between representable virtual double categories.

Example 2.17
.

The virtual double category
\Sigma{\mathbf{M}}
arising from a multicategory
{\mathbf{M}}
is representable if and only if
{\mathbf{M}}
is representable in the sense of
[
Her00
]
, i.e. induced by a monoidal category.

Example 2.18

(
[
GP99
, §3.2]
)
.

{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}
is a pseudo double category. The loose identity associated to a set
A
is the identity span
A=\!=A=\!=A
, and the composite of two spans is given by the pullback of the inner cospan. More generally, for any category
{\mathbf{E}}
with pullbacks,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}({\mathbf{E}})
is a pseudo double category.

Example 2.19

(
[
GP99
, §3.1]
)
.

{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}
is a pseudo double category. The loose identity associated to a small category
\mathbf{A}
is the hom distributor
\mathbf{A}({-},{-})\colon\mathbf{A}{}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{A}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
, and the composite of two distributors
p\colon\mathbf{B}{\mathrel{\to}}\mathbf{A}
and
q\colon\mathbf{C}{\mathrel{\to}}\mathbf{B}
is given by the following coend.

p\odot q\mathrel{:=}\int^{b\in|\mathbf{B}|}p({-},b)\odot q(b,{-})

More generally, for any category
{\mathbf{E}}
with pullbacks and reflexive coequalisers that are stable under pullback,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}({\mathbf{E}})
is a pseudo double category (e.g. see
[
Joh77
, Proposition 2.43]
).

Example 2.20
.

For any pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, we may define a pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}
, the
tight opposite
of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, whose underlying category is
(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}}){}^{\mathrm{op}}
, whose loose morphisms are the same as those of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, and in which a cell with the following frame in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

is given by a cell with the following frame in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

To study exponentiability of virtual double categories in the next section, we observe that the 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
admits binary products, which are given componentwise in the usual manner. Rather than spell this out, we appeal to the following stronger observation.

Proposition 2.21

(
[
DPP06
, Remark 2.14]
)
.

{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is a complete and cocomplete 2-category.

3.
Exponentiable virtual double categories

Our investigation begins with the study of exponentiability in the 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
of virtual double categories. As we shall see,
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is not cartesian closed (
Corollary

3.18
), but there are nevertheless several interesting classes of exponentiable objects. To analyse exponentials of virtual double categories, it will be useful to first spend a little time studying the relationship between virtual double categories and graphs internal to categories.

Definition 3.1
.

Denote by
{\mathbf{G}}\mathrel{:=}\{G_{1}\rightrightarrows G_{0}\}
the free-standing parallel pair.

(3.1.(1))

{\mathbf{Grph}}\mathrel{:=}[{\mathbf{G}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
is the category of graphs.

(3.1.(2))

{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})\mathrel{:=}[{\mathbf{G}},\mathbf{Cat}]
is the 2-category of graphs internal to categories.

There are two perspectives on graphs internal to categories, which we make explicit to avoid potential confusion about our notational conventions.

Proposition 3.2
.

The 2-category
{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
is isomorphic to the 2-category
\mathbf{Cat}({\mathbf{Grph}})
of categories internal to
{\mathbf{Grph}}
.

Proof.

Follows from the fact that limits in functor categories are computed componentwise. Abstractly, this is an instance of the symmetry of internalisation for two-dimensional limit sketches
[
ABK24
, Theorem 7.5]
.
∎

Explicitly, the objects of
{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
comprise graphs of of categories (below left), where
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}
is a category of
objects
and
tight morphisms
, and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}
is a category of
loose morphisms
and
unary cells
. On the other hand, objects of
\mathbf{Cat}({\mathbf{Grph}})
comprise internal categories (below right), where
({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{\top})_{0}
is a graph of objects and loose morphisms, and
({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{\top})_{1}
is a graph of tight morphisms and unary cells. The isomorphism between the two 2-categories may therefore be seen as a kind of transposition operation, analogous to that for strict double categories
[
GP99
, §1.2]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{\top})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{\top})_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{\top})_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{\top})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{\top})}}

In this paper, we will focus on the perspective of
{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
; our notation is chosen to reflect this, as well as to match the typical convention for pseudo double categories viewed as pseudocategories.

Every virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
has an underlying graph
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{{-}}
internal to categories, given by forgetting the non-unary cells. Conversely, we may view a graph
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
internal to categories as a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{{-}}
with no non-unary cells. The precise relationship between categories, graphs internal to categories, and virtual double categories is summarised by the following pair of adjunctions.

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.54852pt}{-5.79442pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-16.04852pt}{-13.82495pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{-15.97908pt}{1.50696pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Proof.

The existence of the leftmost 2-adjunction follows from initiality of the empty category
\mathbf{0}
. The rightmost 2-adjunction

{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{{-}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})\cong{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{{-}})
is elementary. In both cases, the right adjoints are retractions of the left adjoints.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Neither
{\mathbf{0}\rightrightarrows{({-})}}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
nor
\overline{{({-})}}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
admits a left adjoint, since neither preserves the terminal object.

It follows from
Proposition

3.3
that the forgetful functor
\underline{{({-})}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
is given by
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\overline{\mathbf{1}},{-})
, where
\mathbf{1}
is the terminal category. In what follows, we may refer to the underlying category of a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as either
\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}
or
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}
(thereby leaving
{({-})}_{{-}}
implicit), depending on the intuition we wish to convey.

Definition 3.5
.

A virtual double category is
unary
if each of its cells is unary, i.e. it is in the image of
{({-})}^{{-}}\colon{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
.

While exponentiability of general virtual double categories is subtle,
unary
virtual double categories are much better behaved.

Proposition 3.6
.

The 2-category
{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
is cartesian closed. Explicitly, given unary virtual double categories
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, the exponential
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
in
{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
is given by the following unary virtual double category.

•

The underlying category is the functor category
[{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}]
.

•

A loose morphism from
F
to
F^{\prime}
is a graph morphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

Explicitly, this comprises the following data.

(3.6.(1))

For each loose morphism
p\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}Y
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, a loose morphism
\Xi p\colon FX{\mathrel{\to}}GY
.

(3.6.(2))

For each cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{GY^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{FX^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gy}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi p^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi\alpha}

These are required to satisfy the following laws.

(3.6.(3))

\Xi(1_{p})=1_{\Xi(p)}
.

(3.6.(4))

\Xi(\alpha\mathbin{;}\alpha^{\prime})=\Xi(\alpha)\mathbin{;}\Xi(\alpha^{\prime})
.

•

A cell with the following frame

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

is a natural transformation
\xi\colon\Xi\Rightarrow\Xi^{\prime}
that commutes with
\Phi
and
\Gamma
.

Proof.

{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
is a presheaf 2-category, hence is cartesian closed. The explicit description of the exponential is given by evaluating the exponentials in the presheaf 2-category using the Yoneda lemma.
∎

Our strategy for studying exponentiability of general virtual double categories stems from the observation that exponentials of virtual double categories are closely related to exponentials of unary virtual double categories.

Definition 3.7
.

A 2-adjunction
L\dashv R
between cartesian monoidal 2-categories satisfies the
reciprocity condition
if the canonical 1-cell
\langle\varepsilon_{X}\circ L\pi_{1},L\pi_{2}\rangle\colon L(RX\times Y)\to X\times LY
is invertible for all objects
X
and
Y
.

It is a straightforward exercise to verify that a 2-adjunction satisfies the reciprocity condition if and only if the right adjoint preserves exponentials (cf.
[
Law70
, 6]
).

Lemma 3.8
.

The 2-adjunctions of
Proposition

3.3
satisfy the reciprocity condition. Consequently, the 2-functors
{({-})}_{{-}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
and
{({-})}_{0}\colon{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})\to\mathbf{Cat}
, and their composite
\underline{{({-})}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
, preserve exponentials.

Proof.

The reciprocity condition for the first adjunction states that the canonical functor
({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{{-}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})^{{-}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{{-}}
is invertible for each virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
and unary virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
. Since the
n
-ary cells in a product are given by pairs of
n
-ary cells in each component, and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}^{{-}}
has only unary cells, this is clear. The reciprocity condition for the second adjunction states that the canonical functor
({\mathbf{0}\rightrightarrows({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}\times\mathbf{A})})\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\times({\mathbf{0}\rightrightarrows\mathbf{A}})
is invertible for each unary virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
and category
\mathbf{A}
. This is clear for essentially the same reason, since
{\mathbf{0}\rightrightarrows\mathbf{A}}
has no cells whatsoever.
∎

In particular, observe that, upon taking
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{1}
to be the terminal category in the reciprocity condition for the 2-adjunction
\overline{{({-})}}\dashv\underline{{({-})}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
, we have that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\times\overline{\mathbf{1}}\cong\overline{(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}})}
for every virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, i.e. taking the product of any virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
with the virtual double category
\overline{\mathbf{1}}
having a single tight morphism and no loose morphisms forgets the loose morphisms in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

We are now ready to prove the main theorem of this section. As we have mentioned (and will substantiate in
Corollary

3.18
),
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is not cartesian closed. However, there is an important class of exponentiable objects: namely, the pseudo double categories.

Theorem 3.9
.

Representable virtual double categories are exponentiable.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
be a pseudo double category and let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
be a virtual double category. We shall define a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, guided by
Lemma

3.8
, which tells us that the unary virtual double category
({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})_{{-}}
underlying
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
must be
({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{{-}})^{({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{{-}})}
. Thus, it is only necessary to exercise creativity in coming up with the appropriate definition of the multiary cells and composition; the remaining data is determined.

(3.9.(1))

The underlying category is given by
{\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}}^{\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}}}
.

(3.9.(2))

A loose morphism from
F
to
F^{\prime}
is given by a graph morphism as follows (see
Proposition

3.6
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

(3.9.(3))

A cell with the following frame

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{i+1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}A_{0}^{\prime}}}

(3.9.(4))

The identity cell on
\Xi\colon F^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}F
is given by sending each loose morphism
p
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
to the identity cell
1_{\Xi(p)}
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

is given by the cell sending each chain

of loose morphisms in

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}A_{n_{m}}^{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi((p_{1}^{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n_{1}}^{1})\odot\cdots\odot(p_{1}^{m}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n_{m}}^{m}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi(p_{1}^{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n_{1}}^{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{1}^{m}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n_{m}}^{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{1}(p_{1}^{1},\ldots,p_{n_{1}}^{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{m}(p_{1}^{m},\ldots,p_{n_{m}}^{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi(p_{1}^{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n_{1}}^{1},\ldots,p_{1}^{m}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n_{m}}^{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi(\cong)}

That composition is unital and associative follows from unitality and associativity of composition in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, together with naturality of the cells.

We must show that, given a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
, there is an isomorphism of categories
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})
, 2-natural in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
. First, note that this isomorphism certainly holds on the underlying categories of objects and tight morphisms, i.e.
\mathbf{Cat}(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}}\times\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}},\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}})\cong\mathbf{Cat}(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}},\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}^{\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}}})
, so it remains to check assignments on the loose morphisms and cells.

(3.9.(1))

A functor
F\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
comprises an assignment sending pairs of loose morphisms,
p\colon W^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}W
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
and
q\colon A^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}A
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, to a loose morphism
F(p,q)\colon F(W^{\prime},A^{\prime}){\mathrel{\to}}F(W,A)
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
; and an assignment sending pairs of a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
and a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

to a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W_{1},A_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W_{n},A_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W,A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W^{\prime},A^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(w,a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p_{1},q_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p_{n},q_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(w^{\prime},a^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p,q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\omega,\alpha)}

(3.9.(2))

A functor
G\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
comprises an assignment on loose morphisms sending a loose morphism

in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
to a morphism of spans,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(W^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(W)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

which sends each loose morphism

in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
to a loose morphism
G(p)(q)\colon G(W^{\prime})(A^{\prime}){\mathrel{\to}}G(W)(A)
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, and sends each unary cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

to a unary cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W)(A_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W^{\prime})(A_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W)(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W^{\prime})(A^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(W)(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(W^{\prime})(a^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)(q^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)(\alpha)}

It also comprises an assignment of cells
\omega
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
as above to a cell in

with the following frame,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W_{1})({-})}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W)({-})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W^{\prime})({-})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(w)({-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p_{1})({-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p_{n})({-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)({-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\omega)({-})}

hence, for each chain of loose morphisms
