in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W_{1})(A_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W_{n})(A_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W)(A_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W^{\prime})(A_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(w)(A_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p_{1})(q_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p_{n})(q_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(w^{\prime})(A_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)(q_{1}\odot\cdots\odot q_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\omega)(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})}

It is clear that the action of (
3.9.(1)
) and (
3.9.(2)
) above on loose morphisms and unary cells is the same. To establish the one-dimensional universal property of the exponential, it remains only to verify that their action on arbitrary cells is the same.

Observe that, since
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
is representable, every cell
\alpha
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
factors uniquely through an opcartesian cell as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}\odot\cdots\odot q_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{opcart}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\alpha}}

Consequently, given
\omega
and
\alpha
as above, we have by (
3.9.(2)
) a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W_{1})(A_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W_{n})(A_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W)(A_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W^{\prime})(A_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W)(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W^{\prime})(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(w)(A_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p_{1})(q_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p_{n})(q_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(w^{\prime})(A_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(W)(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)(q_{1}\odot\cdots\odot q_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(W^{\prime})(a^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\omega)(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)(\check{\alpha})}

Conversely, for each chain of loose morphisms
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, we have by (
3.9.(1)
) a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W_{1},A_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W_{n},A_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W,A_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(W^{\prime})(A_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(w,A_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p_{1},q_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p_{n},q_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(w^{\prime},A_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p,q_{1}\odot\cdots\odot q_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\omega,{\mathrm{opcart}})}

That this correspondence is bijective follows from opcartesianness.

Finally, we must show that the two-dimensional universal property is satisfied. This is simple, since only unary cells are involved in the data of a transformation (
Definition

2.10
).

(3.9.(1))

A transformation between lax functors
F\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
comprises, for each
p\colon W^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}W
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
and
q\colon A^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}A
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(X,A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(X,A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{X,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p,q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{X^{\prime},A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(p,q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{p,q}}

(3.9.(2))

A transformation between lax functors
G\colon W\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
comprises, for each
p\colon W^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}W
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
, a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
as follows,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(X){({-})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(X^{\prime}){({-})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}(X){({-})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}(X^{\prime}){({-})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p){({-})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}(p){({-})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{p}}

hence, for each loose morphism
q\colon A^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}A
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(X)(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(X^{\prime})(A^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}(X)(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}(X^{\prime})(A^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{X})_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{X^{\prime}})_{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}(p)(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{p})_{q}}

These data, subject to the naturality conditions of
Definition

2.10
and (
3.9.(3)
), are elementarily equivalent. The verification that these assignments are 2-natural is routine.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

The reader familiar with
[
Pis14
]
’s work on exponentiable multicategories
[
Pis14
]
will observe similarities in our analysis of exponentiability for virtual double categories. However, we note that there is a subtlety that arises for virtual double categories that is not present in the theory of multicategories. First, recall from
[
Pis14
, §2.1]
that there is a 2-adjunction as follows, where the left adjoint interprets a category as a multicategory in which every multimorphism is unary.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Multicat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{{-}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}_{{-}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

This adjunction turns out to play both the role of the adjunction
{({-})}^{{-}}\dashv{({-})}_{{-}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})
, as well as the role of the adjunction
\overline{{({-})}}\dashv\underline{{({-})}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
in the theory of virtual double categories. This is because the 2-category
\mathbf{Cat}
appears in the theory of multicategories in two ways.

•

From the perspective of generalised multicategories
[
CS10
]
, it is the 2-category underlying the virtual double category on which the monad parameterising the notion of multicategory is defined (namely, the
strict monoidal category
monad on
{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}
).

•

From the perspective of 2-category theory, it is the base of enrichment.

For virtual double categories, these 2-categories no longer coincide: the former is the 2-category
{\mathbf{Grph}}(\mathbf{Cat})\cong\mathbf{Cat}({\mathbf{Grph}})
, whereas the latter is again
\mathbf{Cat}
. For this reason, when one wishes to extend a given result from multicategories to virtual double categories, it typically takes a little thought to identify the role that
\mathbf{Cat}
is playing in that instance.

Representability is merely a sufficient condition for exponentiability, not a necessary condition. To demonstrate this, we exhibit another class of exponentiable virtual double categories, not contained in the class of representable virtual double categories. We first observe that categories, in addition to being coreflective in virtual double categories by
Proposition

3.3
, are also reflective therein
[
FL25
, §6]
.

Proposition 3.11
.

The forgetful 2-functor
\underline{{({-})}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
admits a fully faithful right adjoint
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
sending each category to the representable virtual double category having the same underlying category, a unique loose morphism between every pair of objects, and a unique cell filling every frame.

Proof.

Trivial.
∎

Since
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}(\mathbf{A})
is representable for each category
\mathbf{A}
, it is exponentiable by
Theorem

3.9
. In addition to the representable virtual double categories, every discrete virtual double category on a category is exponentiable
6
6

6

This has also been observed by
[
FL25
, Remark 6.1]
.
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a category.
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
is exponentiable, but is representable if and only if
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
. Concretely,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}^{\overline{\mathbf{A}}}\cong{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}}^{\mathbf{A}})
for any virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
.

Proof.

We have the following chain of isomorphims, natural in virtual double categories
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
.

(
Lemma

3.8
)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\times\overline{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\overline{\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}\times\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})

(
Proposition

3.3
)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Cat}(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}\times\mathbf{A},\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}})

(
\mathbf{Cat}
is cartesian closed)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Cat}(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}},\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}}^{\mathbf{A}})

(
Proposition

3.11
)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}}^{\mathbf{A}}))

Finally, note that
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
has no loose morphisms, so cannot be representable unless it has no objects (in which case it is trivially representable).
∎

In particular, we have
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\overline{\mathbf{1}}}\cong{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}(\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}})
for every virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, and so the 2-adjunction
{({-})}\times\overline{\mathbf{1}}\dashv{({-})}^{\overline{\mathbf{1}}}
recovers the 2-adjunction
\underline{{({-})}}\dashv{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}
of
Proposition

3.11
.

3.1.
An exponentiability conjecture

It would certainly be of interest to characterise the exponentiable virtual double categories generally. We shall not do so here, as our motivating applications require only the exponentiability of pseudo double categories. However, we shall draw an analogy to the theory of multicategories to make a conjecture. The exponentiable multicategories may be characterised as the promonoidal categories
[
Pis14
, Proposition 2.8]
(i.e. the pseudocomonoids in the monoidal bicategory
\mathbf{Dist}
), which may be viewed as multicategories that are generated from their nullary and binary multimorphisms. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.13
.

A
pro-double category
is a pseudocategory in the bicategory
\mathbf{Dist}
of small categories, distributors, and natural transformations for which the source and target projections are representable distributors.
7
7

7

Abstractly, the inclusion of bicategories
\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{Dist}
transfers finite product sketch structure, and we are defining a pro-double category to be a pseudocategory in this sketch structure on
\mathbf{Dist}
, in the sense of
[
ABK24
]
(modulo the distinction between sketches on 2-categories and sketches on bicategories, which is easily resolved).

Explicitly, this comprises categories
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}
, functors
s,t\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}
, distributors
\mathsf{I}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}{}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
and
{\odot}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}{}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{2}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
together with natural transformations expressing compatibility, and associativity and unitality constraints.

In particular, every probicategory in the sense of
[
Day73
]
(i.e. a
\mathbf{Dist}
-enriched bicategory) gives rise to a pro-double category whose underlying category of objects and tight morphisms is discrete (this is a bicategorical analogue of the classical correspondence between enriched and internal categories
[
Ver92
, §2.2]
). Pro-double categories may be viewed as virtual double categories that are generated from their nullary and binary cells.

In practice, being promonoidal is not a particularly convenient property with which to work. It is therefore useful to identify an intrinsic property of a multicategory that corresponds to its being induced by a promonoidal category. Following
[
Rom24
, Definition 2.3.3 & Proposition 2.3.10]
, we shall refer to the appropriate property as
malleability
: conceptually, it says that the composition operation of a multicategory is invertible, in that every multimorphism may be decomposed in a suitable manner. The following definition captures the analoguous property for virtual double categories.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
T
be the monad on
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}({\mathbf{Grph}})
whose algebras are categories, inducing a monad
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(T)
on
{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}({\mathbf{Grph}})

[
CS10
, Examples 4.7 & 8.13]
. A virtual double category is
malleable
if, viewed as a
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(T)
-monoid
in the sense of
[
CS10
, Definition 4.3]
, its multiplication cell is cartesian.

Finally, recall that the promonoidal categories may be characterised as the cocontinuously monoidal presheaf categories
[
Day70
]
, which, in light of
[
Pis14
]
’s work
[
Pis14
, Proposition 2.12]
, suggests that the cartesian multicategory
{\mathbf{Set}}
plays a distinguished role with respect to exponentiability, analogous to the Sierpiński locale in the category of locales (cf.
[
Tow06
, Theorem 2]
). These observations motivate the following conjecture.

Conjecture 3.15
.

The following are equivalent for a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

(3.15.(1))

{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is exponentiable.

(3.15.(2))

{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is malleable.

(3.15.(3))

{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is a pro-double category.

(3.15.(4))

The exponential
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
exists.

3.2.
Relation to multicategories

{\mathbf{Multicat}}
is a full sub-2-category of
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
: its image comprises the virtual double categories whose underlying category is terminal (i.e. the virtual double categories having a single object and single tight morphism as in
Example

2.3
). Following from the work of
[
Pis14
]
, every representable multicategory (i.e. monoidal category) is exponentiable, since monoidal categories are in particular promonoidal. In fact, the exponentiability of monoidal categories is a special case of a more general phenomenon: that multicategories may be powered by exponentiable virtual double categories.

Proposition 3.16
.

{\mathbf{Multicat}}
is powered over
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}_{\mathrm{exp}}
(the full sub-2-category of
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
spanned by exponentiable virtual double categories) and the 2-functor
\Sigma\colon{\mathbf{Multicat}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
strictly preserves these powers. Explicitly, for a multicategory
{\mathbf{M}}
and pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, the power
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\pitchfork{\mathbf{M}}\mathrel{:=}(\Sigma{\mathbf{M}})^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
has objects the functors
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}\to\mathbf{M}_{1}
and as multimorphisms
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}\to G
families of multimorphisms

\{F_{1}(p_{1}),\ldots,F_{n}(p_{n})\to G(p_{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n})\}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{n}}

natural in
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
. Consequently,
\Sigma
preserves exponentials by monoidal categories.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{M}}
be a multicategory and let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
be an exponentiable virtual double category. Since the underlying category of
\Sigma{\mathbf{M}}
is terminal, so is that of
(\Sigma{\mathbf{M}})^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
by
Lemma

3.8
: we denote the corresponding multicategory
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\pitchfork{\mathbf{M}}
. We therefore obtain a functor
\pitchfork\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}_{\mathrm{exp}}{}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{Multicat}}\to{\mathbf{Multicat}}
. That this exhibits
{\mathbf{Multicat}}
as powered follows from the fact that exponentiation exhibits
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
as powered over
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}_{\mathrm{exp}}
, and
{\mathbf{Multicat}}
is a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
. Unwrapping the description of the exponential in
Theorem

3.9
gives the stated definition of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\pitchfork{\mathbf{M}}
when
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
is representable.

For
\mathbf{A}
a monoidal category, viewed as a representable multicategory,
\Sigma\mathbf{A}
is also representable, and
{\mathbf{M}}^{\Sigma\mathbf{A}}
coincides with
\Sigma({\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{A}})
by inspection of the definition of exponentials of multicategories
[
Pis14
, §2.9]
.
∎

Given a promonoidal category
\mathbf{A}
and a cocomplete monoidal category
{\mathbf{M}}
, the exponential multicategory
{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{A}}
is representable, and the corresponding monoidal structure coincides with
[
Day70
]
’s
[
Day70
]
convolution monoidal structure
[
Pis14
, Proposition 2.12]
. Consequently, the operation of powering a multicategory by a virtual double category may be viewed as a substantial generalisation of convolution for monoidal categories.

We expect
\Sigma
to preserve exponentials generally; this would follow straightforwardly from a concrete description of exponentials following
3.15
. However, we can show that
\Sigma
reflects exponentiability.

Lemma 3.17
.

\Sigma
reflects exponentiability: if
\mathbf{A}
is a multicategory for which
\Sigma\mathbf{A}
is exponentiable as a virtual double category, then
\mathbf{A}
is exponentiable as a multicategory.

Proof.

Suppose the virtual double category
\Sigma\mathbf{A}
is exponentiable. We have:

(
\Sigma
is fully faithful)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Multicat}}({\mathbf{M}}\times\mathbf{A},\mathbf{N})

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\Sigma({\mathbf{M}}\times\mathbf{A}),\Sigma\mathbf{N})

(
\Sigma
preserves products)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\Sigma{\mathbf{M}}\times\Sigma\mathbf{A},\Sigma\mathbf{N})

(
\Sigma\mathbf{A}
is exponentiable)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\Sigma{\mathbf{M}},\Sigma\mathbf{N}^{\Sigma\mathbf{A}})

(
\Sigma
preserves powers)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\Sigma{\mathbf{M}},\Sigma(\Sigma\mathbf{A}\pitchfork\mathbf{N}))

(
\Sigma
is fully faithful)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{Multicat}}({\mathbf{M}},\Sigma\mathbf{A}\pitchfork\mathbf{N})

which exhibits
\Sigma\mathbf{A}\pitchfork\mathbf{N}
as the exponential
\mathbf{N}^{\mathbf{A}}
.
∎

As a consequence, the failure of cartesian closure of multicategories implies the failure of cartesian closure of virtual double categories.

Corollary 3.18
.

{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is not cartesian closed.

Proof.

If every virtual double category were exponentiable, then every multicategory would also be exponentiable by
Lemma

3.17
. However, the only exponentiable multicategories are the promonoidal ones, by
[
Pis14
, Proposition 2.8]
.
∎

It is worth noting that (a special case of) the power structure of
Proposition

3.16
has been previously studied in the literature. In
[
BMZ23
]
,
[
BMZ23
]
show that, given a small pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, the category of set-valued functors from the category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}
, of loose morphisms and cells in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, admits a colax monoidal structure that recovers the usual convolution monoidal structure of
[
Day70
]
when
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
is the delooping of a monoidal category.

Proposition 3.19
.

For a pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, viewing
{\mathbf{Set}}
as a cartesian multicategory, the power
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\pitchfork{\mathbf{Set}}
is weakly representable, and the corresponding colax monoidal structure on the category
{\mathbf{Set}}^{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}}
is the convolution structure of
[
BMZ23
, Theorem 3.5]
.

Proof.

A multimorphism
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}\to G
in the convolution structure of
[
BMZ23
]
is given by a natural transformation

\{\int^{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{n}}{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}(p_{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n},r)\times F_{1}(p_{1})\times\cdots\times F_{n}(p_{n})\to G(r)\}_{r\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}}

which, by the universal property of the coend, is equivalent to a natural family

\{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}(p_{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n},r)\times F_{1}(p_{1})\times\cdots\times F_{n}(p_{n})\to G(r)\}_{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{n},r\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}}

which, by naturality, is equivalent to

\{F_{1}(p_{1})\times\cdots\times F_{n}(p_{n})\to G(p_{1}\odot\cdots\odot p_{n})\}_{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{n},r\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}}

which are precisely the multimorphisms of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\pitchfork{\mathbf{Set}}
.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

We shall not study the representability of exponentials of virtual double categories here, as it is tangential to our interests. However, the observation that the specific exponentials appearing in
Proposition

3.19
are weakly representable suggest it would be worthwhile to investigate the general phenomenon. We conjecture that, for every pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
and locally cocomplete and weakly representable virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, the exponential
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
is again weakly representable. If
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
furthermore satisfies a suitable factorisation property, such as the
AFP condition
of
[
Par13
, Definition 3.1.1]
or the
n
-cylindrical decomposition property
of
[
BMZ23
, Definition 5.1]
(both of which are satisfied, for example, by pseudo double categories with companions and conjoints), and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
admits non-nullary composites, we expect that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
also admits non-nullary composites. As observed in
[
BMZ23
, Example 4.5]
, loose identities rarely exist in exponentials, even assuming
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
admits them. While the absence of loose identities may seem undesirable, we will observe in
Remark

6.4
that this is less restrictive than might at first be imagined.

4.
The virtual double category of loose monads

As outlined in the introduction, we will show in
Section

6
that exponentiation of virtual double categories is closely connected to
[
Par11
]
’s virtual double category of lax functors. To explain the connection, we must recall a second construction on virtual double categories.

Definition 4.1

(
[
Lei04
, Definition 5.3.1]
)
.

Let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
be a virtual double category. The virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
is defined as follows.

(4.1.(1))

An object is a
loose monad
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, comprising an object
|T|\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, a loose morphism
T\colon|T|{\mathrel{\to}}|T|
, and cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\circ_{T}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathsf{I}}_{T}}}

subject to the following equations.

\displaystyle(\circ_{T},1_{T})\mathbin{;}\circ_{T}

\displaystyle=(1_{T},\circ_{T})\mathbin{;}\circ_{T}

\displaystyle({\mathsf{I}}_{T},1_{T})\mathbin{;}\circ_{T}

\displaystyle=1_{T}

\displaystyle(1_{T},{\mathsf{I}}_{T})\mathbin{;}\circ_{T}

\displaystyle=1_{T}

(4.1.(2))

A tight morphism from
(|S|,S,\circ_{S},{\mathsf{I}}_{S})
to
(|T|,T,\circ_{T},{\mathsf{I}}_{T})
is a
loose monad morphism
, comprising a tight morphism
|f|\colon|S|\to|T|
and a cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|S|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|S|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|f|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|f|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

subject to the following equations.

\displaystyle\circ_{S}\mathbin{;}f

\displaystyle=(f,f)\mathbin{;}\circ_{T}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{I}}_{S}\mathbin{;}f

\displaystyle=1_{|f|}\mathbin{;}{\mathsf{I}}_{T}

(4.1.(3))

A loose morphism is a

\displaystyle=(1_{T},\rho_{m})\mathbin{;}\lambda_{m}

A cell is
loose monad transformation

{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

.

)

\displaystyle(f,\phi)\mathbin{;}\lambda_{m}

(

(

Identities and composition is given componentwise.

Since the only kinds of monads we consider in this paper are loose monads, we will typically refer to loose monads simply as
monads
. Many category-like structures arise by applying the
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
construction to a simpler virtual double category. The prototypical example, stemming from observations of
[
Bén67
]
and
[
Bur71
]
, is the virtual double category of categories and distributors.

Example 4.2
.

{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}})\cong{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}
. More generally, for any category
{\mathbf{E}}
with pullbacks,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}({\mathbf{E}}))\cong{\mathbf{\mathbb{D}ist}}({\mathbf{E}})
.

Many more instances of virtual double categories arising from the
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
construction may be found in the work of
[
CS10
]
; we shall see examples of a different flavour shortly.

4.1.
Normality

The
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
construction admits a convenient universal property: it is the cofree
normal
virtual double category construction. This property will be central to our study of lax functors in
Section

6
.

Definition 4.3
.

A
normal
virtual double category is a virtual double category equipped with a choice of loose identities. A
normal functor
strictly preserves the chosen loose identities. Denote by
\mathbf{NVDblCat}
the 2-category of normal virtual double categories, normal functors, and transformations. There is a forgetful 2-functor
{({-})}^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{NVDblCat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
.

Our definition of normal functor may seem overly strict. However, any functor that preserves loose identities up to isomorphism is isomorphic to a functor that preserves loose identities strictly. Thus there is no loss in generality in considering the stricter notion, which allows us to work with stricter universal properties.

Just as every monoid acts on itself, every loose monad is a module for itself: this exhibits
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
as a normal virtual double category.

Lemma 4.4

(
[
CS10
, Proposition 5.5]
)
.

Let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
be a virtual double category. The virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
is normal: the loose identity on a monad
T
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is
T
itself.

In fact,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
is universal in this respect.

Proposition 4.5

(
[
CS10
, Proposition 5.14]
)
.

There is a 2-adjunction as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{NVDblCat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{VDblCat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Note that
[
CS10
]
only establish the existence of a
pseudo
adjunction, since their normal functors preserve loose identities only up to isomorphism. Since we will have need to analyse the unit and counit of this 2-adjunction further in what follows, we find it helpful to provide a self-contained proof of
Proposition

4.5
.

Proof.

First, it is clear that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
is functorial, since its definition is diagrammatic in nature. We define the unit

(1)

\eta_{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime})

at a normal virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
to be the functor sending an object
A
to the loose monad
A(1,1)
; a tight morphism
f\colon A\to B
to the monad morphism
(f,1_{f})
; a loose morphism
p
to the monad module
p
; and a cell
\varphi
to the monad transformation
\varphi
. The functor
\eta_{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is normal, since it sends a loose identity
A(1,1)\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}A
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
to the monad module
A(1,1)\colon A(1,1){\mathrel{\to}}A(1,1)
, which is the identity on the loose monad
A(1,1)
. It is clear that
\eta
is 2-natural with respect to strictly normal functors.

We define the counit

(2)

\varepsilon_{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}

at a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
to be the forgetful functor sending a loose monad
T
with carrier
B
to the object
B
; a monad morphism
(f,\phi)
to the tight morphism
f
; a monad module
m
to the loose morphism
m
; and a monad transformation
\varphi
to the cell
\varphi
.
\varepsilon
is trivially 2-natural.

The left triangle identity says that the carrier of
A(1,1)
is
A
. The right triangle identity says that if we view a loose monad
T
as a monad module
T\colon T{\mathrel{\to}}T
, which forms a loose monad in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})^{\prime}
, its underlying loose monad is precisely
T
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime})^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathbf{Mnd}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(\varepsilon_{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})}

∎

4.2.
Lax-idempotence

The counit of the 2-adjunction in
Proposition

4.5
gives, for each virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
, a forgetful functor
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
, while the unit gives, for each
normal
virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, a normal functor
\eta_{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime})
. We might hope that these are related by adjointness, and indeed this is true
9
9

9

This has been independently observed by
[
FL25
, Remark 7.8]
.
.

Definition 4.6

(
[
CN24
, Theorem 3.15]
)
.

A 2-adjunction
L\dashv R
with unit
\eta
and counit
\varepsilon
is
lax-idempotent
if there is an adjunction
L\eta\dashv\varepsilon L\colon LRL\Rightarrow L
with invertible unit.

Theorem 4.7
.

The 2-adjunction
{({-})}^{\prime}\dashv{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
is lax-idempotent.

Proof.

We must show that, for each normal virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, we have a reflective adjunction as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime})^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The unit of the adjunction is the identity, the carrier of each identity loose monad
A(1,1)
on an object
A
simply being
A
. The counit
{\eta_{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}^{\prime}\circ\varepsilon_{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow 1
is the transformation specified by, for each loose monad
S
on
A
, the loose monad morphism
(1_{A},{\mathsf{I}}_{S})\colon A(1,1)\to S
; and, for each module
p\colon S{\mathrel{\to}}T
, the identity cell on
p
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{A},{\mathsf{I}}_{S})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{B},{\mathsf{I}}_{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{p}}

The left triangle identity expresses that, for each object
A
, the unit of the loose monad
A(1,1)
is the identity. The right triangle identity expresses that, for each loose monad
S
on
A
, the first component of
(1_{A},{\mathsf{I}}_{S})
is the identity.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

Since the 2-adjunction is lax-idempotent, so too is the induced 2-monad
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({-}^{\prime})
on
\mathbf{NVDblCat}
.
10
10

10

Geoff Cruttwell and Michael Shulman have independently observed that the 2-monad
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({-}^{\prime})
is lax-idempotent.
This permits a simple characterisation of the pseudoalgebras for the 2-monad, which we shall show in future work are related to the
exact virtual double categories
of
[
AM24a
, §5]
(cf.
[
AM24a
, Remark 5.4]
).

We record a useful consequence of lax-idempotence.

Definition 4.9
.

A functor of virtual double categories is
fully faithful
if each of the actions on tight morphisms and on cells is invertible.

Corollary 4.10
.

For each normal virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, the unit component
\eta_{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime})
(
1
) is fully faithful.

Proof.

Immediate from
Theorem

4.7
, since it is the left adjoint of a coreflective adjunction.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

If a functor
F\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
between virtual double categories is fully faithful, then so is
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(F)\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})
.

Proof.

The unit preservation condition for a loose monad morphism between identity loose monads implies that it is determined by the tight morphism component. Loose monad transformations are trivially determined by their underlying cells.
∎

Proposition 4.12
.

If a functor
F\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
from a normal virtual double category to a virtual double category is fully faithful, then so is its transpose
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(F)\circ\eta_{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\prime})\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})
.

Proof.

By
Corollaries

4.10
and
4.11
, the transpose is the composite of two fully faithful functors.
∎

4.3.
Slicing over a monad

We now have almost all the properties of the
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
construction that we need for what follows. One further observation will be relevant: when we analyse the relationship between presheaves and discrete fibrations for double categories in
Section

8
, we shall need to understand how the
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
construction interacts with slicing. The slice of a pseudo double category over an object is well known
[
GP99
, §1.7]
; more generally, we may slice over a loose monad in a pseudo double category
[
GP99
, §2.5]
. Since the 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is complete (
Proposition

2.21
), we may similarly slice over loose monads in a virtual double category; observe that a functor of virtual double categories
\bbn 1\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
expresses the data of a loose monad in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
be a virtual double category and let
T
be a loose monad in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
. The
slice virtual double category

{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}/T
is the slice object in the 2-category of virtual double categories, i.e. the following comma object in
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}/T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bbn 1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}

For any object
X\in{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
admitting a loose identity, denote by
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}/X
the slice
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}/X(1,1)
.

When
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is normal (so that all loose identities exist), the slice over an object is the slice in the 2-category of normal virtual double categories, observing that a normal functor
\bbn 1\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
expresses the data of an object in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, and that the forgetful functor
{({-})}^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{NVDblCat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
preserves limits (as will follow, for instance, from
Proposition

5.2
).

We shall be interested in slicing virtual double categories of the form
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
, for which the following lemma will be useful.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
f\colon A\to B
and
a\colon 1\to A
be 1-cells in a 2-category with a terminal object, and suppose that the slice object
B/fa
exists. If
f
is representably fully faithful, then the comma object
A/a
exists if and only if the following pullback exists, in which case they are isomorphic.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A/a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B/fa}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{fa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\it{pb}}}

Proof.

A cone for the pullback comprises a 1-cell
a^{\prime}\colon X\to A
and a cell
\beta
. Since
f
is representably fully faithful, there exists a cell
\alpha
such that
f\alpha=\beta
, exhibiting a cone for the slice object.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fa^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

∎

The following describes the structure of the slice virtual double category in alternative terms, which shows our definition matches that of
[
Kaw25
, Definition 4.30]
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
be a normal virtual double category and let
T
be a loose monad in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
. The following diagram forms a pullback in
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}/T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})/T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\it{pb}}}

Proof.

Immediate from
Lemma

4.14
, since
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
is representably fully faithful by
Corollary

4.10
.
∎

The following strengthens
[
Kaw25
, Corollary 4.34(ii)]
(which additionally assumes that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is normal and admits restrictions along isomorphisms in the sense of
[
CS10
, Definition 7.1]
). It should be compared to the classical result concerning slices of presheaf categories: since
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
is lax-idempotent, it behaves in a suitable sense like a free cocompletion of virtual double categories (namely under
collapses
of monads and their modules, cf.
[
AM24a
, Definition 5.2]
).

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
be a virtual double category and let
T
be a loose monad in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
. There is an isomorphism of virtual double categories as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}/T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})/T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Proof.

{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
is a right 2-adjoint by
Proposition

4.5
, and so preserves slice objects, observing that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(T)\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(\bbn 1)\cong\bbn 1\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
picks out the identity module on the monad
T
, which is
T
itself.
∎

5.
The free normal virtual double category

We continue our short detour from exponentiability to further explore the relationship between normal virtual double categories and virtual double categories. In particular, we observe that, in addition to admitting a right adjoint,
{({-})}^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{NVDblCat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
also admits a left adjoint. Viewing normality as a representability condition for virtual double categories, which are a kind of generalised multicategory
[
Bur71
]
, the existence of the left adjoint
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{NVDblCat}
is suggested by the theory of representability for generalised multicategories
[
Her01
]
(though there is a subtlety, in that normality is only a
partial
representability condition). This construction is implicit in the work of
[
DPP06
]
and was made explicit in the work of
[
FL25
]
.

Definition 5.1

(
[
FL25
, §7.2]
)
.

Let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
be a virtual double category. The virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
is defined as follows.

(5.1.(1))

The underlying category is
\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}}
.

(5.1.(2))

The loose morphisms from
X
to
Y
comprise the loose morphisms
X{\mathrel{\to}}Y
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
and, when
X=Y
, an endomorphism
X(1,1)\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}X
.

(5.1.(3))

A cell with the following frame (where
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},p
are loose morphisms in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, and a dashed arrow denotes a finite chain of endomorphisms
X_{i}(1,1)
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

is given by a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
with the following frame (i.e. given by removing the endomorphisms
X_{i}(1,1)
in the domain).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Furthermore, for each tight morphism
x\colon X\to X^{\prime}
, there is a unique cell with the following frame.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Identities and composites are inherited from
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

Proposition 5.2
.

There is a lax-idempotent and monadic 2-adjunction as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{NVDblCat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{VDblCat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

That
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}
defines a left 2-adjoint is stated in
[
FL25
, §7.2]
. For lax-idempotence, observe that, since
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}\dashv{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
is lax-idempotent by
Theorem

4.7
, it induces a colax-idempotent 2-comonad. A 2-monad that is left-adjoint to a colax-idempotent 2-comonad is lax-idempotent. The
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}^{\prime}
-algebras are consequently characterised in terms of existence of a left-adjoint retraction to the unit
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})^{\prime}
, which corresponds precisely to the normality condition: the left adjoint provides the interpretation of the formal loose identities in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
as loose morphisms in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, as well as the interpretation of cells involving them; and the unit of the adjunction and triangle identities specify the nullary opcartesian cells in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.
∎

Consequently, we have a 2-adjoint triple
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}\dashv{({-})}^{\prime}\dashv{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
, inducing an adjoint 2-monad–2-comonad pair
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}^{\prime}\dashv{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}^{\prime}
. This situation is useful, as we may take advantage of the calculus of adjoint triples, properties of one adjunction being deducible from properties of the other
[
EM65
]
.

Corollary 5.3
.

{({-})}^{\prime}\dashv{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
is 2-comonadic.

Proof.

Follows from 2-monadicity of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}\dashv{({-})}^{\prime}
by the
\mathbf{Cat}
-enriched analogue of
[
EM65
, Proposition 3.3]
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}
does not preserve the terminal object
\bbn 1
, so does not admit a further left adjoint.
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
does not admit a further right adjoint. Suppose that it did, so that we have a natural isomorphism as follows.

\mathbf{NVDblCat}({\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}),{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},R{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})

Taking
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}=\overline{\mathbf{1}}
, and denoting by
\bbn 0=\overline{\mathbf{0}}
the empty virtual double category, we have

\mathbf{1}\cong\mathbf{NVDblCat}(\bbn 0,{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})\cong\mathbf{NVDblCat}({\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(\overline{\mathbf{1}}),{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\overline{\mathbf{1}},R{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})\cong\underline{R{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}}

which implies that
R{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
has a unique object for all
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
. However, taking
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}=\bbn 0
, we have

\mathbf{0}\cong\mathbf{NVDblCat}({\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}),\bbn 0)\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},R\bbn 0)

which implies that
R\bbn 0=\bbn 0
, which is a contradiction.

Remark 5.5
.

The 2-functor
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is similar in nature to the 2-functor
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{{({-})}})^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
, the difference being that the former adjoins a unique loose morphism
!_{a,a^{\prime}}\colon a{\mathrel{\to}}a^{\prime}
between every pair of objects
a,a^{\prime}
, whereas the latter does so only for
a=a^{\prime}
. Consequently, for a category
\mathbf{A}
,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}(\mathbf{A})\cong{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathbf{A}
has at most one object. In particular,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{1}})^{\prime}\cong\bbn 1
, since
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}h}}
is a right adjoint (
Proposition

3.11
) and thus preserves the terminal object.

As we observed in
Proposition

2.21
,
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is known to be complete and cocomplete as a 2-category and hence admit powers and copowers by categories. However, the methodology used to establish this fact relies upon the construction of limits in 2-categories of coalgebras and colimits in 2-categories of algebras
[
DPP06
]
, and therefore does not produce concrete descriptions of either. In contrast, the adjoint triple between virtual double categories and normal virtual double categories gives a concrete description of both powers and copowers.

Proposition 5.6
.

The 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is powered and copowered over
\mathbf{Cat}
. For a category
\mathbf{A}
and virtual double categories
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}\cdot{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}\pitchfork{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}^{{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}}

Proof.

First,
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}
is representable, its only loose morphisms being loose identities, hence is exponentiable by
Theorem

3.9
. We have
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}^{{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}})\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})
. We must show that
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbf{Cat}(\mathbf{A},{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}))
. An object of the latter comprises, for each object
a\in|\mathbf{A}|
, a functor
F_{a}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
and, for each morphism
\alpha\colon a\to a^{\prime}
, a transformation
F_{\alpha}\colon F_{a}\Rightarrow F_{a^{\prime}}
, functorial in
a\in|\mathbf{A}|
.

We obtain such an assignment from a functor
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
by evaluation. For each object
a\in|\mathbf{A}|
, there is a unique (necessarily normal) functor
{!}_{a}\colon\bbn 1\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}
sending the unique object of
\bbn 1
to
a
. Similarly, for each morphism
\alpha\colon a\to a^{\prime}
, there is a unique transformation
{!}_{\alpha}\colon{!}_{a}\Rightarrow{!}_{a^{\prime}}
whose component at the unique object of
\bbn 1
is
\alpha
. This induces a family of functors
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({!}_{a}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\bbn 1\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})
natural in
a\in|\mathbf{A}|
.

Conversely, given
F_{{({-})}}\colon\mathbf{A}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})
, we define a functor
F_{({-}_{1})}({-}_{2})\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
. For the assignment on loose morphisms, observe that loose morphisms in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}
are in bijection with objects of
\mathbf{A}
. Therefore, loose morphisms in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
are indexed by an
object
of
\mathbf{A}
and a
loose morphism
of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, thus making this assignment well defined.

It remains to verify that these assignments are inverse to one another and natural in each variable, which is a straightforward calculation.
∎

It follows from
Proposition

5.6
that the embedding
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{{({-})}})^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
plays an analogous role for virtual double categories that the cocartesian (a.k.a. sequential) multicategory construction plays for multicategories
[
Pis13
,
Pis14
]
. For instance, the following is an analogue of the fact that the cocartesian multicategory construction is left-adjoint to the category of monoids construction
[
Pis14
, Corollary 3.10]
.

Proposition 5.7
.

The 2-functor
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{{({-})}})^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is fully faithful and left-adjoint to
\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}{({-})}^{\prime}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
.

Proof.

First, full faithfulness follows because the action on loose morphisms is uniquely determined. Adjointness is given by composition of the 2-adjunctions of
Propositions

3.3
,
4.5
and
5.2
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{VDblCat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{VDblCat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{({-})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{({-})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}{({-})}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}{({-})}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

∎

Remark 5.8
.

Since
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is copowered over
\mathbf{Cat}
, we obtain, for each fixed virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, a 2-adjunction
{({-})}\cdot{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}\dashv{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{-})\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
.

(5.8.(1))

When
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}=\overline{\mathbf{1}}
, this recovers the free–forgetful 2-adjunction
\overline{{({-})}}\dashv\underline{{({-})}}
of
Proposition

3.3
.

(5.8.(2))

When
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}=\bbn 1
, this recovers the 2-adjunction
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{{({-})}})^{\prime}\dashv\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}{({-})}^{\prime}}
of
Proposition

5.7
.

Therefore,
\mathbf{Cat}
is embedded coreflectively in
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
in two ways, identifying the categories as both the ‘loosely empty’ virtual double categories (having no loose morphisms) and the ‘loosely discrete’ virtual double categories (having only loose identities); and, by
Proposition

3.11
, is embedded reflectively by identifying the categories as the ‘loose codiscrete’ virtual double categories. Note that
\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{{({-})}})^{\prime}}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{Cat}
is isomorphic to the identity, so that
\underline{{({-})}}\colon{\mathbf{VDblCat}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
provides a retraction of both coreflections.

6.
Lax functors as monads

We are now in position to explain the relationship between exponentiation for virtual double categories and the virtual double category of lax functors introduced by
[
Par11
]
. As a warm up, we observe that the category of monads and monad morphisms in a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is given by the category of functors from the terminal virtual double category
\bbn 1
into
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\bbn 1,{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})\cong\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Immediate from the fact that
\bbn 1\cong\mathbb{F}(\overline{\mathbf{1}})^{\prime}
(
Remark

5.5
) together with
Proposition

5.7
.
∎

The following observation, which extends the above from an isomorphism of categories to an isomorphism of virtual double categories, provides our original motivation for considering exponentiation of virtual double categories.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
be a pseudo double category and let
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
be a virtual double category. The virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
of
[
Par11
, Theorem 4.3]

11
11

11

[
Par11
]
only defined
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
under the additional assumption that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
is representable, but the definition extends in the evident way.
is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})
.

Proof.

First, we observe that the underlying categories are isomorphic. Using
Lemma

6.1
for the final isomorphism, we have the following.

\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})}\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\bbn 1,{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})\cong\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})^{\prime}}

Next, we must check the data of the loose morphisms and cells. A loose morphism in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})
is given by a module between loose monads. Explicitly, it is given by a loose morphism in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, i.e. a graph morphism, equipped with two cells in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
corresponding to the left and right actions. Comparing with the data and axioms for a module in
[
Par11
, Definition 3.2]
, (M1 & M2) express the data of a graph morphism; (M3) expresses the data of the left and right actions; (M4) expresses functoriality of the graph morphism (see
•
‣
Proposition

3.6
); (M5) expresses naturality of the left and right actions; (M6) expresses the compatibility of the right action with the multiplication, the compatibility of the left action with the right action, and the compatibility of the left action with the multiplication; (M7) expresses the compatibility of the right action with the unit, and the compatibility of the left action with the unit.

A cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})
is given by a cell in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
satisfying several compatibility conditions. Comparing with the data and axioms for a multimodulation in
[
Par11
, Definition 4.2]
, (mm1) expresses the data of a cell; (mm2) expresses naturality; (mm3
l
) expresses to the compatibility condition for the left action with itself; (mm3
r
) expresses to the compatibility condition for the right action with itself; (mm3
i
) expresses to the compatibility condition for the left action with the right action; (mm3
0
) expresses the nullary compatibility condition.

[
Par11
]
does not define composition of cells in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
, therein deferring them to
[
Par13
]
, where they are also not defined explicitly. However, it is clear that they must coincide with those of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}})
.
∎

Beyond being purely of conceptual interest, this observation has many practical consequences. For one, it provides a simpler construction of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
that makes concrete computations substantially easier (e.g.
Section

6.1
). For another, it provides a universal property of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
that means that concrete computations are often rendered unnecessary (e.g.
Section

6.2
). In the remainder of the paper, we will explore some of these consequences.

First, normality of the virtual double category of lax functors follows as immediate corollary of
Theorem

6.2
.

Corollary 6.3

(
[
Par11
,
Par13
]
)
.

{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
is normal.

Proof.

Immediate from
Theorems

6.2
and
4.4
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

If
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
admits non-nullary composites, restrictions, and local reflexive coequalisers, then
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
is representable: this is stated in
[
CS10
, Theorem A.1]
under the further assumptions that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
admits arbitrary composites and local coequalisers, but inspection of the proof reveals the stronger assumptions to be unnecessary. It is straightforward to show that, if
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
admits restrictions and local reflexive coequalisers, then so too does each exponential
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
. Consequently, supposing the conjecture in
Remark

3.20
holds, it would follow that, given a pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
satisfying a suitable factorisation condition and a locally cocomplete pseudo double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
with restrictions, the virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
is representable (and is furthermore locally cocomplete with restrictions), which would recover
[
Par13
, Theorem 4.0.1]
. Our expectation is that this proof strategy would result in a substantial simplication of the proof ibid., given the relative simplicity of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
compared to
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})
.

In the following subsections, we will explore some of the more interesting consequences of
Theorem

6.2
.

6.1.
Enrichment of double categories

The reader of
[
Par11
]
may be led to wonder whether the assignment
({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})\mapsto{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
of pairs of pseudo double categories to the normal virtual double category of lax functors between them defines an enrichment of pseudo double categories in normal virtual double categories. While verifying this by hand is not a difficult exercise, it does involve some tedious calculations. On the other hand, it is a straightforward corollary of
Theorem

6.2
. We require a preparatory lemma.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
(\mathscr{D},\otimes,I)
be a symmetric monoidal bicategory and let
{\mathscr{C}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}
be a full sub-bicategory. Suppose that, for each
A,B\in{\mathscr{C}}
, the internal hom
[A,B]
exists in
\mathscr{D}
. Then
{\mathscr{C}}
is canonically equipped with a
\mathscr{D}
-enriched structure.

Proof.

The enriched structure on
{\mathscr{C}}
is defined in the same way as the canonical enriched structure on a closed monoidal bicategory
[
Lac95
, Proposition 3.9.6]
, with
{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)\mathrel{:=}[A,B]
. Full faithfulness ensures that the underlying bicategory of the enriched bicategory coincides with
{\mathscr{C}}
, since
\mathscr{D}(I,[A,B])\simeq\mathscr{D}(A,B)\simeq{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)
.
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

The 2-category
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}
of pseudo double categories and lax functors admits the following enrichments.

(6.6.(1))

In the cartesian monoidal 2-category
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
via
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})\mathrel{:=}{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
.

(6.6.(2))

In the cartesian monoidal 2-category
\mathbf{NVDblCat}
via
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})\mathrel{:=}{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
.

Proof.

First, the inclusion
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}\to{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
is a fully faithful 2-functor (
Theorem

2.15
). (1) thus follows from
Lemma

6.5
using that representable virtual double categories are exponentiable (
Theorem

3.9
). For (2), we use change of base along
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
, using that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
is right-adjoint and so preserves products, along with the characterisation of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
in
Theorem

6.2
. It is clear that the underlying 2-category is precisely
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}
, since
\mathbf{NVDblCat}(\bbn 1,{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}}))\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{B}}})
.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

Bicategories enriched in the cartesian monoidal 2-category
\mathbf{NVDblCat}
are generalisations of the locally cubical bicategories of
[
GG09
, Definition 11]
, which are bicategories enriched in the cartesian monoidal 2-category
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{p}
of pseudo double categories and pseudo functors. Note that each of the 2-functors in the following commutative square preserves products and so, by change of base for enriched bicategories
[
GS16
, §13.2]
, every locally cubical bicategory induces bicategories enriched in
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}
,
\mathbf{NVDblCat}
, and
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
. However, only change of base along the vertical functors below preserves underlying 2-categories (since normal functors from
\bbn 1
pick out objects, whereas non-normal functors from
\bbn 1
pick out loose monads).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{NVDblCat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{VDblCat}}}

6.2.
Skew-closed structure

The universal properties of exponentiation and of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}
supply
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}
with a universal property of its own.

Lemma 6.8
.

For each normal virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
, exponentiable virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, and virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, we have a 2-natural isomorphism of categories:

{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})\cong\mathbf{NVDblCat}({\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}))

Consequently, for each virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}}
, exponentiable virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, and virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, we have a 2-natural isomorphism of categories:

{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(({\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})^{\prime})

Proof.

For the first isomorphism, we use
Theorem

6.2
together with the following 2-adjunction.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{NVDblCat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{VDblCat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{VDblCat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

For the second isomorphism, we precompose the adjunction
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}\dashv{({-})}^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

In particular, for a category
\mathbf{A}
and virtual double categories
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
and
{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}
, we have the following chain of natural isomorphisms,

(
Lemma

6.8
)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbf{VDblCat}}(\overline{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})^{\prime})

(
Proposition

3.3
)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Cat}(\mathbf{A},\underline{{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}})^{\prime}})

(
Theorem

6.2
)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Cat}(\mathbf{A},{\mathbf{VDblCat}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{Y}}}))

recovering the description of the copower
\mathbf{A}\cdot{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
in
Proposition

5.6
. However, note that this does not give an alternative proof of
Proposition

5.6
, as it only establishes the universal property for
exponentiable

{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
.

Lemma

6.8
indicates that
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({-},{-})
forms something resembling a closed monoidal structure on the 2-category of virtual double categories. Since not every virtual double category is exponentiable, such a monoidal structure will be at best partially closed, but we may still hope that the monoidal structure itself is totally defined. This is, in fact, very close to being the case: while the closed structure expressed by
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({-},{-})
does not correspond to a monoidal structure, it does correspond to a
left-skew monoidal
structure in the sense of
[
Szl12
]
.

Theorem 6.10
.

The assignment
({\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}})\mapsto({\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}})^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
equips
{\mathbf{VDblCat}}
with the structure of a right-normal left-skew monoidal 2-category
({\mathbf{VDblCat}},\olessthan,\bbn 1)
. Furthermore, for each exponentiable virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, we have
{({-})}\olessthan{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}\dashv{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}},{-})^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Every 2-monad admits a unique colax monoidal structure with respect to cartesian monoidal structure, and so the given assignment defines a left-skew monoidal structure by the two-dimensional analogue of
[
LS12
, Example 3.8]
. Right-normality follows because
({\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}})^{\prime}\times\bbn 1\cong({\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}{\mathbf{\mathbb{W}}})^{\prime}
. That this skew-monoidal structure is right-closed with respect to exponentiable virtual double categories follows from
Lemma

6.8
.
∎

Corollary 6.11
.

The 2-category
{\mathbf{DblCat}}_{l}
is equipped with right-normal left-skew monoidal structure
(\olessthan,\bbn 1)
.

Proof.

It suffices to check that
\olessthan
restricts to pseudo double categories. This is trivial, as
{\mathbf{\mathbb{F}}}
preserves representability, as does the cartesian product.
∎

7.
Presheaves for double categories

In this section, we examine some implications of
Theorem

6.2
for the virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{L}ax}}({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}},{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}})
of presheaves on a double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
, which was studied extensively by
[
Par11
]
. We begin by spelling out the definition of the exponential
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}^{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}}
, which admits some simplification over the general case.

Definition 7.1
.

For a virtual double category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
, denote by
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}_{n}
the category whose objects are chains of loose morphisms of length
n
, and whose morphisms comprise
n
cells in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{X}}}
with compatible source and target.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{n}}

Example 7.2
.

Observe that the category
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}_{1}
of spans and span morphisms is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{Set}}^{\{\cdot\leftarrow\cdot\to\cdot\}}
, the category of
{\mathbf{Set}}
-valued functors from the free-standing span, and that the inclusion
1\to\{\cdot\leftarrow\cdot\to\cdot\}
that picks out the middle object of the span induces a functor
m\colon{\mathbf{Set}}^{\{\cdot\leftarrow\cdot\to\cdot\}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
.

The underlying category of
{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}^{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}{}^{\mathrm{op\,t}}}
is the presheaf category
{\mathbf{Set}}^{({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}){}^{\mathrm{op}}}
. A loose morphism from
F
to
F^{\prime}
is given by a functor and a pair of natural transformations as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{t}}

A cell with the following frame (left) is given by a natural transformation (right).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{n}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{n}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}_{1}}}

Above, we have denoted by
{\odot}\circ\langle\Xi_{1},\ldots,\Xi_{n}\rangle
the functor sending a chain of loose morphisms
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}
to the limit (i.e. iterated pullback) of the following chain of spans.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Xi_{1}(p_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Xi_{2}(p_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}(X_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}(X_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}(X_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}(X_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{t}(p_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{s}(p_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{t}(p_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{s}(p_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{t}(p_{3})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{s}(p_{n})}

The identity cell on
\Xi
is given by the identity natural transformation on
\Xi\colon({\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{0}){}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Composition is given as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{n}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}pan}}_{1}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\odot{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\Xi_{1}^{1},\ldots,\Xi_{m_{n}}^{n}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\odot{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\Xi_{1},\ldots,\Xi_{n}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\odot}
